- The reader thread does everything that needs no exchange state: parsing, authentication, field validation, rate and in-flight limits. Nothing it refuses is journalled.
- The writer thread stamps, journals (one fsync per batch), then applies each command to the Ledger and calls the listener before completing the request's future.
- Private lines (FILL, ORDER, CANCELLED, CANCEL_REJECT) carry a per-participant number, `oseq`, and go to every session of that participant; RESEND repeats retained ones.
- Market data (TRADE, LEVEL) goes only to subscribers and carries the command sequence number.
- Recovery = snapshot (if the journal names one) + replay of the journal since it; CHECK records prove the replay matched the live run.
- Every exit path has a defined result: 0 clean stop (signals included), 1 startup or tool failure, 2 halt.

## Common confusions

- **"The reply goes to the session that sent the order."** Since D73 it goes to every session logged in as that participant, numbered in its private stream. Only refusals (BUSY, RATE_LIMITED, …) go to the sending session alone.
- **"The callback sends the ORDER line."** No: the listener does; the callback only reports refusals and keeps the in-flight count (D76).
- **"oseq and seq are the same counter."** `seq` numbers journal records for the whole exchange; `oseq` numbers one participant's private events.

## Questions to test yourself

1. Which three facts does every flow rely on?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>One writer thread owns all exchange state; write-ahead (fsync before processing); the listener runs before the request's future completes (D76).</details>

2. Where does a RATE_LIMITED refusal come from, and is it journalled?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>From the session's reader thread (`ClientSession.admit`), before anything is queued; it is never journalled.</details>

3. What line tells a client the latest number of its private stream when it logs in?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>`WELCOME participant=<p> oseq=<latest>`; it comes from an Attach read on the writer thread, so no event can fall between it and the first live event.</details>


---

<a id="guide-05a-core-flows-trading"></a>

<!-- source: docs/understanding/05a-core-flows-trading.md -->

# 05a — Core flows, part 1: trading, cancels, market data, resend

> **Project:** ExchangeServer2 | Single-instrument limit order book: price-time matching engine, write-ahead journal with replay, single writer thread, TCP/TLS gateway with authenticated participants | Stack: Java 17, Maven wrapper, JUnit 5 (+ JMH, PIT)
> **This guide:** Step-by-step traces of flow 1 (login and a NEW order that trades), flow 2 (CANCEL, CANCEL_ALL and cancel-on-disconnect) and flow 3 (SUBSCRIBE/UNSUBSCRIBE and RESEND), with the data shape at each step | **Prerequisites:** 01, 05 (helpful: 02, 06) | **Related:** 05b, 06, 07-modules/gateway, 07-modules/exchange-service, 07-modules/exchange-and-replay, 07-modules/engine, 07-modules/book
> **Repo layout summary:** `pom.xml` + Maven wrapper (`mvnw`, `mvnw.cmd`) at the root; production code in `src/main/java/exchangeserver/` (packages `domain`, `book`, `engine`, `command`, `journal`, `core`, `gateway`, `client`, plus `Main`); tests mirror it in `src/test/java/exchangeserver/` (plus `bench/` JMH benchmarks and `testing/MutableClock`); design docs in `docs/` (`ExchangeServer2_Vision.md` = purpose/roadmap, `ExchangeServer2_Spec.md` = decisions D1–D77); these guides in `docs/understanding/`.

## Overview

This guide follows client requests through a running exchange, from the bytes a client sends to the lines it gets back, naming every method and file touched. Terms used throughout (also in guide 13):

- **Participant**: a trading identity (`alice`). A **session** is one TCP connection logged in as a participant; a participant may have several.
- **Private stream** (D73): the participant's own events (replies to its requests, fills on its resting orders, cancels), numbered 1, 2, 3, … by **oseq**, sent to *every* session of that participant.
- **Market data**: TRADE and LEVEL lines, sent to sessions that subscribed; they carry the command **seq** (the journal's sequence number), not an oseq.
- **Ledger**: the writer thread's derived state, rebuilt as one unit: the engine (book and counters), the running **output digest** (D70) and the **output log** that numbers private events (D73).
- **In flight**: a request a session has handed to the exchange and whose callback has not run yet (D75, D76).

### The three threads a request crosses

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    subgraph session["one TCP connection"]
        R["reader thread<br/>session-N-reader"]
        S["sender thread<br/>session-N-sender"]
    end
    W["exchange-writer<br/>(one for the whole exchange)"]
    R -- "Request + Pending (queue)" --> W
    W -- "lines offered to bounded queues" --> S
    R -- "refusals, PONG, ERROR" --> S
```

**[Verified]** thread names: `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:91-92`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/ExchangeService.java:119`.

### The running example (seen live, 2026-10-27, Windows)

Two users were created with `--add-user` (PBKDF2 hashes in `exchange.users`), the server was started, and four raw TCP clients sent these lines. **[Verified]** captured output:

```
mallory > LOGIN alice guessed-secret
mallory < ERROR login failed
mallory < (closed)
alice > LOGIN alice alice-secret COD
alice < WELCOME participant=alice oseq=0
bob > LOGIN bob bob-secret!
bob < WELCOME participant=bob oseq=0
alice > NEW a1 SELL LIMIT 10 100
alice < ORDER oseq=1 orderId=1 clOrdId=a1 status=RESTED filled=0 remaining=10
alice > NEW a2 BUY LIMIT 5 100
alice < ORDER oseq=2 orderId=2 clOrdId=a2 status=CANCELLED filled=0 remaining=5 reason=SELF_TRADE_PREVENTED
bob > NEW b1 BUY LIMIT 4 100
bob < FILL oseq=1 orderId=3 clOrdId=b1 side=BUY price=100 qty=4 leaves=0 liquidity=TAKER
bob < ORDER oseq=1 orderId=3 clOrdId=b1 status=FILLED filled=4 remaining=0
alice < FILL oseq=3 orderId=1 clOrdId=a1 side=SELL price=100 qty=4 leaves=6 liquidity=MAKER
alice > QUIT
alice < BYE
bob > SUBSCRIBE
bob < SUBSCRIBED
bob < BOOK seq=4 last=100 bids=- asks=-
alice2 > LOGIN alice alice-secret
alice2 < WELCOME participant=alice oseq=4
alice2 > RESEND 1
alice2 < ORDER oseq=1 orderId=1 clOrdId=a1 status=RESTED filled=0 remaining=10 resend=1
alice2 < ORDER oseq=2 orderId=2 clOrdId=a2 status=CANCELLED filled=0 remaining=5 reason=SELF_TRADE_PREVENTED resend=1
alice2 < FILL oseq=3 orderId=1 clOrdId=a1 side=SELL price=100 qty=4 leaves=6 liquidity=MAKER resend=1
alice2 < CANCELLED oseq=4 orderId=1 qty=6 reason=CANCEL_ALL resend=1
alice2 < RESENT last=4
```

And the journal it left (each record ends with its CRC-32C, D69):

```
HEADER,3,2,0,5d7826e0
NEW,1,2026-10-27T14:20:40.373493200Z,1,alice,a1,SELL,LIMIT,10,100,379beca7
NEW,2,2026-10-27T14:20:40.802670Z,2,alice,a2,BUY,LIMIT,5,100,f33577be
NEW,3,2026-10-27T14:20:41.217763100Z,3,bob,b1,BUY,LIMIT,4,100,bed0c339
CANCEL_ALL,4,2026-10-27T14:20:42.045241200Z,alice,2b6bb4a5
```

Read the story: mallory's guess is refused and disconnected; alice logs in asking for cancel-on-disconnect (`COD`); her buy a2 would have traded with her own sell a1, so self-trade prevention cancels it; bob's buy trades 4 against a1; when alice quits, her last session is gone, so the gateway submits a journalled `CANCEL_ALL` (record 4) that removes the rest of a1; when she logs in again, `WELCOME oseq=4` tells her four events exist, and `RESEND 1` repeats all of them.

---

## Flow 1 — Login, then a NEW order that trades

**Trigger:** a client sends `LOGIN bob bob-secret!`, then `NEW b1 BUY LIMIT 4 100` while alice's `a1 SELL 10 @ 100` rests. **Final effect:** a NEW record is fsynced; the book changes; bob's sessions get `FILL … TAKER` and `ORDER … FILLED` (his oseq 1); alice's sessions get `FILL … MAKER` (her oseq 3); subscribers get TRADE and LEVEL.

### Sequence diagram

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant C as bob's client
    participant R as bob's session reader
    participant Q as ExchangeService queue
    participant W as exchange-writer (Exchange.process)
    participant J as CommandJournal
    participant L as Ledger (engine, digest, log)
    participant G as TcpGateway.onOutput
    participant SB as bob's session sender(s)
    participant SA as alice's session sender(s)
    C->>R: LOGIN bob bob-secret!
    R->>R: Credentials.verify (PBKDF2), idle timeout set
    R->>Q: Attach(bob) read
    Q->>W: batch
    W->>R: callback on writer: gateway.loggedIn, WELCOME oseq=0
    C->>R: NEW b1 BUY LIMIT 4 100
    R->>R: parse, OrderRequest, admit() (in-flight, rate)
    R->>Q: Submit + Pending (tracked callback)
    Q->>W: batch
    W->>J: appendAll (one write, one fsync)
    W->>L: apply(NewOrderCommand)
    L-->>W: output + private events [bob#1 OrderReply, alice#3 MakerFill]
    W->>G: onOutput(output, events)
    G->>SB: FILL oseq=1 TAKER, ORDER oseq=1
    G->>SA: FILL oseq=3 MAKER
    G->>G: TRADE, LEVEL to subscribers
    W->>W: complete future, callback: no refusal, in-flight - 1
```

### Step by step

**Step 1 — the session exists.** The acceptor thread accepted the socket, set `TCP_NODELAY`, refused it if 256 sessions were open, and started a `ClientSession` with a sender and a reader thread. If the server has a TLS keystore, the server socket is an `SSLServerSocket` and the handshake happens on the reader's first read. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/TcpGateway.java:113-129` (TLS server socket), `TcpGateway.java:164-205` (accept loop).

**Step 2 — reading a line with a login timeout.** The reader sets a socket read timeout of `loginTimeout` (10 s by default) before reading anything, and reads byte by byte up to `\n` (a trailing `\r` is dropped; over 256 bytes is an error). **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:152-182`, `ClientSession.java:186-204`.

*Data shape:* the text line `LOGIN bob bob-secret!`.

**Step 3 — parse.** `Protocol.parse` splits on single spaces and builds a `ClientMessage`: here `Login("bob", "bob-secret!", false)`; a fourth token `COD` sets `cancelOnDisconnect`. `ClientMessage.Login.toString()` leaves the secret out, so a logged message never shows it. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/Protocol.java:60-100`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientMessage.java:12-17`.

**Step 4 — authenticate (D74).** `login` allows one attempt per connection; checks the name pattern and the secret against `Credentials`; on any failure it answers the same `ERROR login failed` and stops reading (the session closes after its in-flight requests, of which there are none). On success the read timeout becomes `idleTimeout` (300 s), and an **Attach read** is handed to the exchange. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:293-327`.

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:303-326 (trimmed)
String requested = login.participant();
if (!Rules.PARTICIPANT.matcher(requested).matches() || !gateway.authenticate(requested, login.secret())) {
    send(Protocol.error("login failed"));       // the same answer whatever was wrong (D74)
    return false;
}
socket.setSoTimeout((int) settings.idleTimeout().toMillis());
cancelOnDisconnect = login.cancelOnDisconnect();
participant = requested;
// On the writer thread, before any later output: register for routing, then report the latest oseq (D73).
service.attach(requested, tracked((lastOseq, failure) -> {
    if (failure != null) {
        replyFailure("LOGIN", failure);
        return;
    }
    gateway.loggedIn(this);
    if (closed.get()) {
        gateway.removed(this);
        return;
    }
    send(Protocol.welcome(requested, lastOseq));
}));
```

`Credentials.verify` runs PBKDF2-HMAC-SHA256 with the participant's salt and iteration count (210,000 for users created with `--add-user`) and compares in constant time; an unknown participant is checked against a dummy entry so it costs the same. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/Credentials.java:82-87`.

*Why an Attach read, not a direct registration?* The Attach request goes through the queue like a command, so its callback runs on the writer thread *between* two commands: the session is registered for routing and told the latest oseq at one point in the stream. Every private event after it reaches the session, none before it is counted twice: no gap, no duplicate (D73). **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Request.java:43-47`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Exchange.java:255-261`; **[Inferred]** the no-gap argument (it mirrors the one for SUBSCRIBE, flow 3).

**Step 5 — `NEW b1 BUY LIMIT 4 100`: parse and validate.** The reader now handles a `NewOrder`. Before anything reaches the exchange: the session must be logged in; `admit()` applies the per-session limits (D75); then `OrderRequest.of` applies every stateless field rule (D47). **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:236-253`, `ClientSession.java:329-340`.

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:279-291
private String admit() {
    if (inFlight.get() >= settings.maxInFlight()) {
        return "SESSION_BUSY";
    }
    long now = System.nanoTime();
    tokens = Math.min(settings.burst(), tokens + (now - lastRefillNanos) * settings.ratePerSecond() / 1e9);
    lastRefillNanos = now;
    if (tokens < 1) {
        return "RATE_LIMITED";
    }
    tokens -= 1;
    return null;
}
```

`admit` is a token bucket: tokens refill at `ratePerSecond` (1,000) up to `burst` (2,000); each request spends one. It reads the clock (`System.nanoTime`), which is fine: it runs on the reader thread, outside the engine, so replay determinism is untouched. A refusal is an `ERROR clOrdId=b1 RATE_LIMITED` (or `SESSION_BUSY`) line and nothing is queued or journalled. **[Verified]** code; regression tests `TcpGatewayTest.Limits.requestsBeyondTheRateAreRefusedWithoutReachingTheJournal`, `requestsBeyondTheInFlightLimitAreRefused`.

*Data shape:* `ClientMessage.NewOrder("b1", BUY, LIMIT, 4, OptionalLong.of(100))` → `OrderRequest{participant=bob, clOrdId=b1, BUY, LIMIT, qty 4, price 100}`.

**Step 6 — hand-in with a tracked callback.** `service.submit(request, tracked(...))`. `tracked` increments the session's in-flight count now and decrements it after the callback has run; the callback itself only reports a refusal, because the FILL and ORDER lines come through the private stream. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:337-339`, `ClientSession.java:421-431`.

`ExchangeService.enqueue` wraps the callback so a throw inside it is logged (M9), then, under one lock, refuses with HALTED/STOPPED/BUSY or adds the `Pending` to the queue. A refusal completes the future on the reader thread, outside the lock. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/ExchangeService.java:211-238`.

*Data shape:* `Pending{request = Request.Submit(OrderRequest), result = CompletableFuture<Response>, command = null, refused = false}` in a `LinkedBlockingQueue`.

**Step 7 — the writer takes a batch.** It blocks on `take()`, then drains whatever else is waiting, up to 1,000 (smart batching, D52). **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/ExchangeService.java:286-305`.

**Step 8 — stamp (and maybe CHECK).** `Exchange.process` first stamps a `CHECK` record if `checkInterval` (10,000) commands have been applied since the last one; it carries the current output digest and so asserts "every command before me produced these outputs" (D70). Then each request is stamped by the `Sequencer`, the only clock reader and counter: bob's order becomes `NewOrderCommand(seq 3, ts, orderId 3, request)`. A request whose stamping throws is marked `refused` and never journalled. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Exchange.java:198-221`.

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Exchange.java:199-223 (trimmed)
List<Command> commands = new ArrayList<>(batch.size() + 1);
if (commandsSinceCheck >= options.checkInterval()) {
    commands.add(sequencer.stampCheck(ledger.digest()));       // D70: covers every command before it
    commandsSinceCheck = 0;
}
for (Pending<?> p : batch) {
    try {
        if (p.request instanceof Request.Submit s) {
            p.command = sequencer.stampNewOrder(s.order());
        } else if (p.request instanceof Request.Cancel c) {
            p.command = sequencer.stampCancel(c.participant(), c.orderId());
        } else if (p.request instanceof Request.CancelAll c) {
            p.command = sequencer.stampCancelAll(c.participant());
        }
    } catch (RuntimeException invalid) {
        p.refused = true;                   // refused before stamping: uses up no number, never journalled
        p.fail(invalid);
        continue;
    }
    if (p.command != null) {
        commands.add(p.command);
    }
}

journal.appendAll(commands);                // durable before any effect
```

**Step 9 — journal: one write, one fsync.** `CommandJournal.appendAll` encodes every command first (a failing encode writes nothing), writes them in one buffer and calls `force(true)`. Each record is sealed with `,<crc32c>\n` (D69). A failed write or fsync fail-stops the journal and halts the writer (flow 6). **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/journal/CommandJournal.java:143-166`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/journal/JournalCodec.java:166-168`.

*Data shape on disk:* `NEW,3,2026-10-27T14:20:41.217763100Z,3,bob,b1,BUY,LIMIT,4,100,bed0c339`.

**Step 10 — apply to the Ledger.** For each non-refused request: a read (no command) is answered from the ledger at once; a command goes through the test seam (`interceptor`, a no-op in production) and `ledger.apply`, which runs the engine, folds the output into the digest, and numbers the private events. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Exchange.java:225-252`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Ledger.java:42-55`.

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Ledger.java:42-55
Applied apply(Command command) {
    EngineOutput output;
    if (command instanceof NewOrderCommand n) {
        output = engine.onNewOrder(n);
    } else if (command instanceof CancelOrderCommand c) {
        output = engine.onCancel(c);
    } else if (command instanceof CancelAllCommand c) {
        output = engine.onCancelAll(c);
    } else {
        throw new IllegalStateException("Not an engine command: " + command);
    }
    digest.update(output);
    return new Applied(output, log.record(command, output));
}
```

**Step 11 — the engine matches.** `MatchingEngine.onNewOrder` checks the order id and sequence number increase (D35), builds the `Order`, and runs `process` in three parts. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/engine/MatchingEngine.java:111-125`, `MatchingEngine.java:178-263`.

1. *Checks that change nothing* (`MatchingEngine.java:182-214`): duplicate client order id (the id is recorded even if a later check rejects); price band ±10% around the last trade (none before the first trade); **resting caps only for an order that would rest** (D68: at the participant cap or with 1,000,000 orders in the book, a LIMIT that would fully trade now is still accepted; POST_ONLY is rejected because it never trades on arrival); FOK fillability; POST_ONLY would cross.
2. *The match loop* (`MatchingEngine.java:216-237`): while the order has quantity, take the best opposite level; stop if it does not cross the order's limit; **stop if the head of that level belongs to the same participant (self-trade prevention, D67)**; otherwise `book.fillBest` fills the head order by up to the remainder and returns an immutable `Fill`, and the engine records a `Trade` (at the resting price, with the incoming command's timestamp) and an `Execution` for the maker.
3. *Outcome* (`MatchingEngine.java:239-262`): an exhaustive switch over the order type. A LIMIT/POST_ONLY remainder rests unless self-trade prevention stopped the loop, in which case it is CANCELLED with reason `SELF_TRADE_PREVENTED`; IOC and MARKET remainders are CANCELLED (with that reason if it applied); a FOK cannot have a remainder here.

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/engine/MatchingEngine.java:218-228 (trimmed)
boolean selfTradePrevented = false;
while (order.getRemainingQuantity() > 0) {
    Optional<PriceLevel> best = book.bestLevel(restingSide);
    if (best.isEmpty() || !crosses(order.getSide(), limit, best.get().getPrice())) {
        break;
    }
    if (order.getParticipant().equals(book.headParticipant(restingSide))) {
        selfTradePrevented = true;      // D67: stop here; the remainder is cancelled below
        break;
    }
    Fill fill = book.fillBest(restingSide, order.getRemainingQuantity());
```

In the running example: `a2 BUY 5 @ 100` meets `a1` (alice's own) at the head of the 100 ask, so it stops before trading and is CANCELLED with the reason; `b1 BUY 4 @ 100` meets `a1`, which is not bob's, and fills 4. **[Verified]** live transcript; tests `MatchingEngineTest.SelfTrade.*`.

The market collar (D50, D66) is the limit of a MARKET order: ±10% around the last trade, or, before the first trade, around the best opposite price (the touch), so a first market order cannot sweep a thin book. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/engine/MatchingEngine.java:274-289`.

*Data shape:* `OrderOutput(seq 3, ts, Response(orderId 3, "b1", FILLED, reason null, filled 4, remaining 0, [Trade(100, 4, buy 3, sell 1, BUY, ts)]), passiveFills [Execution(alice, 1, "a1", SELL, 100, 4, leaves 6)], levelUpdates [LevelUpdate(SELL, 100, 6, 1)])`.

**Step 12 — digest and numbering.** `OutputDigest.update` feeds a canonical text encoding of the output (every field: response, trades, executions, level updates, cancel results) into a running FNV-1a 64 hash (D70). `OutputLog.record` turns the output into numbered private events, requester's reply first, then the makers' fills in match order, and keeps the last `outputRetention` (1,000) per participant for RESEND. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/OutputDigest.java:48-83`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/OutputLog.java:58-76`.

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/OutputLog.java:58-65 (trimmed)
List<PrivateEvent> record(Command command, EngineOutput output) {
    List<PrivateEvent> events = new ArrayList<>();
    if (output instanceof OrderOutput o && command instanceof NewOrderCommand n) {
        String p = n.request().getParticipant();
        events.add(add(new PrivateEvent.OrderReply(p, next(p), n.request().getSide(), n.request().getQuantity(), o.response())));
        for (Execution e : o.passiveFills()) {
            events.add(add(new PrivateEvent.MakerFill(e.participant(), next(e.participant()), e)));
        }
```

*Data shape:* `[OrderReply(bob, oseq 1, BUY, 4, Response…), MakerFill(alice, oseq 3, Execution…)]`. Alice is at 3 because her a1 reply was 1 and her a2 reply was 2.

**Step 13 — listener, then the future (D76).** `Exchange.process` calls `listener.onOutput(output, events)` and only afterwards completes the request's future, which runs the callback (on the writer thread). A throwing listener is logged and ignored. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Exchange.java:245-251`.

**Step 14 — the gateway routes.** `TcpGateway.onOutput` sends each private event's lines to every session registered for its participant, then, if anyone subscribed and the output has trades or level updates, the TRADE and LEVEL lines to every subscriber. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/TcpGateway.java:285-310`.

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/TcpGateway.java:286-296 (trimmed)
public void onOutput(EngineOutput output, List<PrivateEvent> events) {
    for (PrivateEvent e : events) {
        Set<ClientSession> owners = sessionsByParticipant.get(e.participant());
        if (owners != null) {
            List<String> lines = Protocol.lines(e, false);
            for (ClientSession s : owners) {
                s.sendAll(lines);
            }
        }
    }
```

`Protocol.lines` turns an `OrderReply` into one taker FILL per trade (with `leaves` counting down from the order quantity) followed by the ORDER line, all with the same oseq; a `MakerFill` into one MAKER FILL. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/Protocol.java:145-166`.

`ClientSession.send` only *offers* the line to a bounded queue (10,000 lines); if it is full, the session is disconnected instead of blocking the writer (D54). **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:116-124`.

**Step 15 — the callback.** `p.complete(response)` runs bob's tracked callback: no failure, so nothing is sent; in-flight goes down by one, and if the reader had stopped and this was the last request in flight, the session closes now (flow 6). **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:421-442`.

**Step 16 — the sender writes.** Each session's sender thread takes lines from its queue, writes them with `\n`, and flushes when the queue is empty (once per burst). **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:446-466`.

### Why the taker's lines come before the maker's and before market data

Within one command, `OutputLog.record` lists the requester's reply first and makers' fills after it, and `onOutput` sends private events before market data; all of this runs on the one writer thread, which offers lines to the session queues in that order. Each session's sender preserves its queue order. So bob sees FILL then ORDER, and a subscriber sees TRADE then LEVEL, never the reverse. Across different sessions, delivery order depends on the sender threads and the network. **[Verified]** code order; **[Inferred]** the cross-session statement.

### Variations of flow 1 (same path, different exit)

| Situation | Where it exits | What the client sees |
|---|---|---|
| Wrong secret or unknown name | step 4 | `ERROR login failed`, connection closed (one attempt per connection) |
| No LOGIN for 10 s | step 2 | `ERROR login timeout; disconnecting`, closed |
| Syntax error | step 3 | `ERROR <reason>`, session continues |
| Not logged in | step 5 | `ERROR LOGIN first` |
| Over the rate or in-flight limit | step 5 | `ERROR clOrdId=… RATE_LIMITED` / `SESSION_BUSY` |
| Invalid field (quantity 0, bad clOrdId) | step 5 | `ERROR clOrdId=… <message>` |
| Exchange queue full / stopping / halted | step 6 | `ERROR clOrdId=… BUSY` / `STOPPED` / `HALTED` |
| Duplicate clOrdId, band, caps, post-only cross | step 11 | `ORDER … status=REJECTED reason=…` (journalled, numbered) |
| Own order at the head | step 11 | `ORDER … status=CANCELLED … reason=SELF_TRADE_PREVENTED` |
| The engine throws (a bug) | step 10 | `ERROR clOrdId=… INTERNAL_ERROR`; the command is quarantined (flow 5) |

**[Verified]** the code paths cited in each step; the first, fourth and seventh rows were also seen live or in `TcpGatewayTest`.

---

## Flow 2 — CANCEL, CANCEL_ALL and cancel-on-disconnect

### 2a. CANCEL

**Trigger:** `CANCEL 1` from a session logged in as alice. **Final effect:** a CANCEL record is journalled; if order 1 rests and is alice's, it leaves the book (its level too, if emptied), alice's sessions get `CANCELLED oseq=… orderId=1 qty=…`, subscribers get a LEVEL update; otherwise she gets `CANCEL_REJECT oseq=… orderId=1 reason=UNKNOWN_ORDER`.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant R as alice's reader
    participant W as exchange-writer
    participant E as MatchingEngine / OrderBook
    participant G as TcpGateway
    R->>R: parse CANCEL 1, id > 0, admit()
    R->>W: Request.Cancel(alice, 1)
    W->>W: stampCancel, appendAll + fsync
    W->>E: onCancel -> book.cancel(alice, 1)
    E-->>W: CancelOutput(CancelResult, levelUpdates)
    W->>G: onOutput: CancelReply(alice, oseq) + LEVEL
```

1. The reader parses `Cancel(1)`, refuses an id ≤ 0 locally, applies `admit()`, and hands in `cancel(p, id, tracked(...))`. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:254-259`.
2. The writer stamps `CancelOrderCommand(seq, ts, alice, 1)` and journals it. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Exchange.java:208-209`.
3. `MatchingEngine.onCancel` checks the sequence number and calls `OrderBook.cancel(participant, id)`. The book returns `UNKNOWN_ORDER` if the id is not resting **or belongs to someone else** (D48: a distinct "not yours" answer would reveal which ids are live). Otherwise it removes the node through the single removal path, which also removes an emptied level and decrements the participant's open-order count. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/engine/MatchingEngine.java:127-133`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/book/OrderBook.java:98-108`, `OrderBook.java:111-128`.
4. `OutputLog.record` numbers a `CancelReply` for the *canceller* (even an UNKNOWN_ORDER answer is part of its stream); the gateway formats `CANCELLED oseq=… orderId= qty=` or `CANCEL_REJECT oseq=… orderId= reason=UNKNOWN_ORDER`. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/OutputLog.java:66-67`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/Protocol.java:180-184`.

### 2b. CANCEL_ALL and cancel-on-disconnect (D72)

**Trigger:** the last session of a participant that logged in with `COD` closes, for any reason (QUIT, end of stream, slow-consumer disconnect, gateway shutdown). **Final effect:** a `CANCEL_ALL,<seq>,<ts>,<participant>` record is journalled; every resting order of that participant leaves the book; one `CancelReply(byCancelAll=true)` per order is numbered into its private stream (waiting for RESEND); subscribers get LEVEL updates.

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/TcpGateway.java:246-262 (trimmed)
boolean[] wasLast = {false};
sessionsByParticipant.computeIfPresent(p, (k, mine) -> {
    mine.remove(session);
    wasLast[0] = mine.isEmpty();
    return mine.isEmpty() ? null : mine;
});
ExchangeService s = service;
if (wasLast[0] && session.cancelOnDisconnect() && s != null) {
    s.cancelAll(p, (results, failure) -> {
        if (failure != null) {
            LOG.log(Level.WARNING, "cancel-on-disconnect for " + p + " failed: " + failure);
        }
    });
}
```

1. `ClientSession.close()` calls `TcpGateway.removed`, which drops the session from the participant's set atomically (`computeIfPresent`) and learns whether it was the last one. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:133-141`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/TcpGateway.java:239-263`.
2. The writer stamps `CancelAllCommand` and journals it like any input; replay re-runs it. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Exchange.java:210-211`.
3. `MatchingEngine.onCancelAll` collects the participant's order ids by walking the book (bids best first, then asks, time priority within a level: a deterministic order, never a hash map), then cancels each. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/engine/MatchingEngine.java:136-155`.
4. Each result becomes a `CancelReply` with `byCancelAll`, formatted `CANCELLED oseq=… orderId= qty= reason=CANCEL_ALL`. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/OutputLog.java:68-71`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/Protocol.java:182`.

In the running example alice's QUIT left no other alice session, so record 4 cancelled the 6 left of a1, and her reconnected session saw it as oseq 4 through RESEND. Without `COD`, orders stay (test `TcpGatewayTest.CancelOnDisconnect.withoutCodOrdersStay`). **[Verified]** live run and tests.

*Clean shutdown:* the shutdown hook closes the gateway first (every session closes, so COD participants trigger CANCEL_ALL), then stops the service; requests accepted before `stop()` are processed, so COD orders are cancelled on a clean stop. A crash cancels nothing. **[Inferred]** from the hook order (`src/main/java/exchangeserver/Main.java:159-177`) and D56; not exercised by a test.

---

## Flow 3 — SUBSCRIBE, UNSUBSCRIBE and RESEND (reads through the queue)

Reads do not change state, but they still go through the writer's queue: only the writer may touch the book or the output log (single writer, D57), and the queue gives each read an exact position between commands.

### 3a. SUBSCRIBE and UNSUBSCRIBE

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant R as reader
    participant W as exchange-writer
    participant G as TcpGateway
    R->>R: lock: active = true, mine = ++number
    R->>W: Snapshot(depth 100) read, tracked callback
    W->>W: engine.snapshot(100)
    W->>R: callback on writer: lock, still current? SUBSCRIBED, BOOK, gateway.subscribe
    Note over W,G: every later command's TRADE/LEVEL reaches this session
```

1. `subscribe` takes `subscriptionLock`, refuses if already active, marks it active and takes a new subscription number, then queues a depth-100 snapshot read (D62). **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:373-384`.
2. The writer answers the read from `engine.snapshot(100)`; the callback runs on the writer, between commands. Under the lock it checks the number is still current and the subscription still active (an UNSUBSCRIBE may have overtaken it), then sends `SUBSCRIBED`, `BOOK seq= last= bids= asks=` and joins the subscribers. Because this happens at one point in the command stream, the first LEVEL the session gets belongs to the first command after the snapshot: no gap, no duplicate. **[Verified]** `ClientSession.java:385-402`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Exchange.java:255-258`.
3. `UNSUBSCRIBE` (reader thread, under the same lock) clears the flag, leaves the subscribers and sends `UNSUBSCRIBED`. **[Verified]** `ClientSession.java:405-411`.

The lock and the number handle two races: an UNSUBSCRIBE arriving before the snapshot callback (the callback then sends nothing), and SUBSCRIBE/UNSUBSCRIBE/SUBSCRIBE (only the current number's callback acts, so one BOOK). **[Verified]** comment `ClientSession.java:363-372`; test `TcpGatewayTest.MarketData.rapidSubscribeUnsubscribeEndsConsistent`.

In the running example bob subscribed after alice's CANCEL_ALL: `BOOK seq=4 last=100 bids=- asks=-` (the book is empty; the last trade, 100, remains the band reference). **[Verified]** live.

### 3b. RESEND (D73)

**Trigger:** `RESEND <n>` from a logged-in session. **Final effect:** the session gets every retained private event of its participant with oseq ≥ n, each line marked `resend=1`, then `RESENT last=<latest>`; if n is older than the oldest retained event, first `ERROR RESEND_GAP first=<oldest>`.

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:348-360 (trimmed)
service.resend(p, from, tracked((ResendResult result, Throwable failure) -> {
    if (failure != null) {
        replyFailure("RESEND", failure);
        return;
    }
    if (from < result.firstAvailable() && from <= result.lastOseq()) {
        send(Protocol.error("RESEND_GAP first=" + result.firstAvailable()));
    }
    for (PrivateEvent e : result.events()) {
        sendAll(Protocol.lines(e, true));
    }
    send("RESENT last=" + result.lastOseq());
}));
```

1. The reader refuses n ≤ 0, applies `admit()`, and queues a `Request.Resend(participant, n)` read. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:260-261`, `ClientSession.java:342-347`.
2. On the writer, `OutputLog.since(participant, n)` returns the retained events with oseq ≥ n, the first retained number and the latest number. The log keeps the last `outputRetention` (1,000) events per participant; after a restart they are rebuilt by replay, but a compaction keeps only the numbers, not the events (D71). **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/OutputLog.java:84-97`, `OutputLog.java:50-54`.
3. The callback sends the events in order, before any later live event (it runs on the writer at its queue position). Events produced between the WELCOME and the RESEND may arrive twice (once live, once resent); the client deduplicates by oseq. **[Verified]** code; **[Inferred]** the duplicate case.

*Data shape:* `ResendResult(events [OrderReply#1, OrderReply#2, MakerFill#3, CancelReply#4], firstAvailable 1, lastOseq 4)`.

Tests: `TcpGatewayTest.Resend.aReconnectingClientRecoversAMakerFillItMissed`, `eventsTooOldToRetainAreReportedAsAGap`, `theStreamSurvivesARestart`. **[Verified]**

---

## Key takeaways

- A session must log in with a secret (PBKDF2, one attempt per connection); WELCOME reports the participant's latest oseq from an Attach read, placed exactly in the command stream.
- The reader thread refuses everything it can without exchange state: syntax, fields, rate (token bucket) and in-flight limits; refusals are never journalled.
- The writer stamps (CHECK first when due), journals with one fsync, applies each command to the Ledger (engine + digest + numbered events), calls the listener, and only then completes the future (D76).
- Private events go to every session of the participant, numbered by oseq; the requester's reply comes before the makers' fills, and private lines before market data.
- Self-trade prevention stops the match loop at the participant's own resting order and cancels the remainder with `SELF_TRADE_PREVENTED`.
- CANCEL returns UNKNOWN_ORDER for someone else's order; CANCEL_ALL is a journalled command, and cancel-on-disconnect submits it when a COD participant's last session closes.
- SUBSCRIBE and RESEND are reads through the queue: each sees one exact point of the history, so no gap or duplicate within it.

## Common confusions

- **"The ORDER line is sent by the request's callback."** It is sent by the listener from the private stream; the callback only reports refusals and decrements the in-flight count.
- **"A second session of alice does not see the first session's replies."** It does: the private stream goes to every session of the participant (D73).
- **"Self-trade prevention skips my order and trades with the next one."** It stops; skipping would break price-time priority (D67).
- **"RESEND can reach any old event."** Only the last 1,000 per participant, and never past the last compaction; older requests get `RESEND_GAP`.
- **"A refused request still uses a sequence number."** Gateway refusals never reach the writer; a request that fails stamping is marked `refused` and uses up no number.

## Questions to test yourself

1. Alice is logged in on two sessions. Session 1 sends `NEW x BUY LIMIT 1 90`. What does session 2 see?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>The same `ORDER oseq=<n> … clOrdId=x …` line: the reply is a private event for participant alice and goes to every alice session (D73).</details>

2. Bob's buy at 101 crosses alice's ask at 100 (head of the level) and bob's own ask at 100 behind it. What happens to a 5-lot when alice's order has 2?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>It trades 2 with alice, then the head of the level is bob's own order: matching stops, and the remaining 3 is CANCELLED with `SELF_TRADE_PREVENTED`.</details>

3. Why is the RATE_LIMITED check allowed to read the clock when the engine may not?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>It runs on the session reader thread before anything is journalled; its result is never an input to replay, so determinism is unaffected.</details>

4. Alice logged in with COD and has two resting orders. Her only session drops. What appears in the journal, and how does she learn about it later?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>A `CANCEL_ALL,<seq>,<ts>,alice,<crc>` record; two `CancelReply` events numbered into her stream. When she logs in again, `WELCOME oseq=` shows the higher number and `RESEND <n>` repeats `CANCELLED … reason=CANCEL_ALL resend=1` lines.</details>

5. An UNSUBSCRIBE arrives on the reader before the snapshot callback of the preceding SUBSCRIBE runs. What does the client hear?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>Only `UNSUBSCRIBED`: the callback sees the subscription withdrawn (under the lock) and sends nothing.</details>


---

<a id="guide-05b-core-flows-lifecycle"></a>

<!-- source: docs/understanding/05b-core-flows-lifecycle.md -->

# 05b — Core flows, part 2: startup, recovery, compaction, poison pills, shutdown

> **Project:** ExchangeServer2 | Single-instrument limit order book: price-time matching engine, write-ahead journal with replay, single writer thread, TCP/TLS gateway with authenticated participants | Stack: Java 17, Maven wrapper, JUnit 5 (+ JMH, PIT)
> **This guide:** Step-by-step traces of flow 4 (startup, crash recovery, compaction, crossing a rules change), flow 5 (poison pills, live and at startup) and flow 6 (shutdown, halt, backpressure, timeouts, closing a connection), with the data shape at each step | **Prerequisites:** 01, 05a (helpful: 06) | **Related:** 05a, 06, 07-modules/journal, 07-modules/exchange-and-replay, 07-modules/exchange-service, 07-modules/client-and-main, 08-cross-cutting
> **Repo layout summary:** `pom.xml` + Maven wrapper (`mvnw`, `mvnw.cmd`) at the root; production code in `src/main/java/exchangeserver/` (packages `domain`, `book`, `engine`, `command`, `journal`, `core`, `gateway`, `client`, plus `Main`); tests mirror it in `src/test/java/exchangeserver/` (plus `bench/` JMH benchmarks and `testing/MutableClock`); design docs in `docs/` (`ExchangeServer2_Vision.md` = purpose/roadmap, `ExchangeServer2_Spec.md` = decisions D1–D77); these guides in `docs/understanding/`.

## Overview

Guide 05a followed requests through a running exchange. This guide covers how the exchange *gets* running, how it rebuilds itself after a crash, how it keeps its history bounded, what it does when its own engine throws, and how it stops. Terms (also in guide 13):

- **Journal**: the append-only text file of stamped commands (`HEADER`, then `NEW` / `CANCEL` / `CANCEL_ALL` / `SKIP` / `CHECK`, each ending in a CRC-32C). With the snapshot it names, it is the only exchange state on disk.
- **Snapshot** (D71): `<journal>.snapshot-<seq>`, the state after command `seq`; the journal header's **base sequence** names the snapshot it continues from (0: none).
- **Replay**: feeding the journalled commands, in order, through a fresh **Ledger** (engine + output digest + output log); outputs are not delivered.
- **CHECK** (D70): a journal record carrying the digest of every output before it; replay must reproduce it.
- **Torn tail**: bytes after the last `\n`, left by a crash in the middle of a write.
- **Poison pill / quarantine / SKIP**: a journalled command that makes the engine throw; a `SKIP` record tells replay to pass over it.
- **Halt / fail-stop**: the writer stops for good after a failure it cannot continue from; the process exits 2.

---

## Flow 4 — Startup, crash recovery, compaction

**Trigger:** `java -jar target/exchange-server2-2.0.0-SNAPSHOT.jar --port 9000 --journal exchange.journal --users exchange.users`. **Final effect:** the book, counters, last trade price, client-order-id map, output digest and every participant's oseq numbering are what they were after the last journalled command; new commands continue the numbering; clients can log in.

### Sequence diagram

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant M as Main (main thread)
    participant G as TcpGateway
    participant S as ExchangeService
    participant X as Exchange
    participant J as CommandJournal
    participant R as Replay
    participant W as exchange-writer
    M->>M: Credentials.load(users), optional TLS context
    M->>G: new TcpGateway(settings): bind (127.0.0.1 by default)
    M->>G: startAccepting(): sessions answer "starting"
    M->>S: ExchangeService.start(journal, gateway, settings)
    S->>X: Exchange.open(file, clock, interceptor, options)
    X->>J: open: JournalLock, header check (adopt rules if empty), cut torn tail
    X->>R: Replay.run: snapshot named by the base, verify, feed + CHECKs
    R-->>X: Result(ledger, lastSeq, lastOrderId, lastTs, fed, skipped, base)
    X->>X: Sequencer.resume, Recovery summary
    S->>W: writer.start()
    M->>G: attach(service): sessions can log in
    M->>M: print listening + recovery lines, shutdown hook, awaitTermination
```

### Step by step

**Step 1 — arguments and tools (`Main.main`).** Options: `--port`, `--bind`, `--journal`, `--users`, `--tls-keystore`, the session limits, `--check-interval`, `--resend-window`; the tools `--add-user`, `--quarantine`, `--compact` run and exit. An unknown option or a missing value prints usage and exits 1. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/Main.java:66-118`.

**Step 2 — credentials, TLS, bind.** `serve` loads the users file (missing file: a message telling the operator to run `--add-user`), builds a TLS context if `--tls-keystore` is given (password from `ES2_TLS_PASSWORD`), warns when binding a non-loopback address without TLS, and creates the gateway, which binds at once (127.0.0.1 unless `--bind`). Any failure here exits 1. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/Main.java:120-138`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/TcpGateway.java:113-129`.

**Step 3 — accept before replay (L6).** `gateway.startAccepting()` starts the acceptor *before* the journal is replayed; until `attach`, a session answers any request with `ERROR exchange is starting; retry shortly` and closes, instead of leaving the client in silence. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/Main.java:139`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:228-232`; test `TcpGatewayTest.Session.beforeTheExchangeIsAttachedSessionsAreToldItIsStarting`.

**Step 4 — `ExchangeService.start` → `Exchange.open` (main thread).** Opening and replaying run on the calling thread; only then is the writer started (`Thread.start()` hands the replayed state to it with a happens-before edge). `Exchange.open` creates the journal, ledger and sequencer itself and closes the journal if anything fails. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/ExchangeService.java:124-136`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Exchange.java:116-137`.

**Step 5 — `CommandJournal.open`: lock, check, change.** **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/journal/CommandJournal.java:75-127`.

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/journal/CommandJournal.java:98-116 (trimmed)
private static CommandJournal openLocked(Path file, int rulesVersion, JournalLock ownedLock) throws IOException {
    JournalCodec.Header header = checkHeader(file, rulesVersion);
    int adoptedFrom = 0;
    if (header != null && header.rulesVersion() != rulesVersion) {
        adoptedFrom = header.rulesVersion();            // checkHeader allowed it: no records yet
        writeFresh(file, rulesVersion, header.baseSequence());
        header = new JournalCodec.Header(rulesVersion, header.baseSequence());
    }

    FileChannel channel = FileChannel.open(file, CREATE, WRITE, APPEND);    // APPEND: never overwrite history
    long truncated;
    try {
        truncated = cutTornTail(file);
        if (channel.size() == 0) {
            channel.write(ByteBuffer.wrap(JournalCodec.encodeHeader(rulesVersion, 0).getBytes(UTF_8)));
            channel.force(true);
            Durable.fsyncDirectory(file);           // a new file's directory entry must be durable too
```

1. **Lock first** (D65, `JournalLock.acquire`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/journal/JournalLock.java:37-66`): add the lock file's real path to an in-JVM set (`HELD`), then `tryLock()` the whole of `<journal>.lock` through a channel nothing else ever opens. A second open, in this JVM or another process, is refused with "already open". The separate file matters on Linux/macOS, where closing *any* handle on a locked file drops the process's locks on it. **[Verified]** on Windows and Linux (cross-process test passed in a Linux container; live `--quarantine` against a running server refused on both).
2. **Check the header before changing anything** (D64, `checkHeader`, `CommandJournal.java:241-260`): a complete first record must be a valid v3 header (checksum included). A header of *other* rules is accepted only if the journal has no records yet: the header is then rewritten atomically with this build's rules, keeping the base sequence (D71: "adopted"). Anything else, including a file that is not a journal, is refused and left untouched.
3. **Cut a torn tail** after the last `\n` and fsync the cut (D38).
4. **Write a header** if the file is empty, fsync it and fsync the directory (D69).

*Data shape:* a `CommandJournal{file, channel (APPEND), lock, baseSequence, truncatedBytesOnOpen, adoptedRulesFrom, failed=false}`.

**Step 6 — `Replay.run`: snapshot, verify, feed.** **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Replay.java:39-48`.

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Replay.java:39-48
static Result run(CommandJournal journal, Path journalFile, long stopBefore, CommandInterceptor interceptor,
                  int retention) throws IOException {
    Snapshot snapshot = snapshotFor(journal, journalFile);
    Verifier verifier = new Verifier(snapshot);
    journal.read(verifier);
    Ledger ledger = Ledger.from(snapshot, retention);
    long fed = feed(journal, ledger, stopBefore, verifier.skipped, interceptor);
    return new Result(ledger, verifier.lastSequenceNumber, verifier.lastOrderId, verifier.lastTimestamp, fed,
            Set.copyOf(verifier.skipped), journal.baseSequence());
}
```

- *Snapshot* (`Replay.java:59-69`): base 0 means the empty snapshot; otherwise `<journal>.snapshot-<base>` is read (each line checksummed, an END record proving it is complete) and must hold that sequence number.
- *Pass 1, verify* (`Replay.java:101-130`): sequence numbers exactly `base+1, base+2, …`; timestamps never decrease; remember the last sequence number, timestamp, last order id from NEW records (skipped ones included: those ids were issued) and the set of skipped sequence numbers. Nothing is fed if any of this fails.
- *Pass 2, feed* (`Replay.java:72-99`): start a `Ledger` from the snapshot (engine restored with time priority, digest and oseq numbering resumed), then feed every NEW/CANCEL/CANCEL_ALL below `stopBefore` that is not skipped. At each CHECK record, the replayed digest must equal the recorded one, or replay stops with `ReplayException(DIVERGED, seq)` (D70). An engine exception becomes `ReplayException(ENGINE_FAILURE, seq)`.

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Replay.java:80-95 (trimmed)
if (command instanceof CheckCommand check) {
    if (!check.digest().equals(ledger.digest())) {
        throw new ReplayException(ReplayException.Kind.DIVERGED, seq, "Replay diverged before CHECK " + seq
                + ": the journal recorded output digest " + check.digest() + ", this build produced "
                + ledger.digest() + ". This build processes the old commands differently; start it from a "
                + "snapshot written by the build that wrote this journal (--compact).", null);
    }
    return;
}
try {
    interceptor.beforeEngine(command);      // the test seam stands in for the engine here
    ledger.apply(command);
} catch (RuntimeException e) {
    throw new ReplayException(ReplayException.Kind.ENGINE_FAILURE, seq, "Replay failed on command " + seq
            + " (" + e.getMessage() + ")", e);
}
```

Because the engine depends only on its commands (no clock, counters, randomness or hash order, D12), the same commands rebuild the same book, last trade price, client-order-id map, digest and oseq numbers. **[Verified]** code; tests `ExchangeTest.Restart.*`, `ExchangeTest.Checkpoints.*`, `TcpGatewayTest.Resend.theStreamSurvivesARestart`.

*Data shape:* `Replay.Result(ledger, lastSequenceNumber, lastOrderId, lastTimestamp, fed, skipped, baseSequence)`.

**Step 7 — resume the sequencer.** It continues at `lastSequenceNumber + 1` and `lastOrderId + 1`, and never stamps a timestamp earlier than the last journalled one. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/command/Sequencer.java:40-46`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Exchange.java:122-123`.

**Step 8 — start the writer, attach, report (L1).** `ExchangeService.start` starts the non-daemon `exchange-writer`; `Main` attaches the service to the gateway (now LOGIN works) and prints two lines: where it listens, and what recovery did. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/Main.java:150-157`. Seen live after a compaction:

```
ExchangeServer2 listening on 127.0.0.1:9137, journal ...\live3\exchange.journal
Recovered through sequence 4 from snapshot 4: replayed 0 commands, cut 0 torn bytes
```

The summary also names quarantined commands and a rules adoption when there are any. **[Verified]** live; `Exchange.Recovery` at `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Exchange.java:78-80`.

**Step 9 — hook and wait.** `Main` registers the shutdown hook (flow 6) and blocks in `service.awaitTermination()`. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/Main.java:159-183`.

### Startup failures

Any failure in steps 4–7 closes the gateway, prints `Startup failed: <message>` and a hint chosen by `Main.hint`, and exits 1 (L2, D77): an `ENGINE_FAILURE` gets the `--quarantine <seq>` advice, a `DIVERGED` gets the compaction advice, anything else (locked journal, other rules with records, not a journal, corrupt record, checksum mismatch, CRLF conversion, sequence gap, missing snapshot) gets "The journal was not changed." **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/Main.java:140-149`, `Main.java:186-198`; tests `MainTest`.

### Crash recovery, seen running

Earlier the same day (before D69 changed the format), the server was hard-killed, half a record appended, and the server restarted: the torn bytes were cut, the book and counters came back, and new commands continued the numbering. With D77 the cut is now reported in the recovery line. **[Verified]** live (format v2 then); `ExchangeTest.Operations.recoveryReportsWhatItDid` checks the report.

### What a crash can and cannot lose

| Crash moment | After restart | Client's view |
|---|---|---|
| Before the batch's fsync returned | Records missing or torn (cut) | Nobody was answered: consistent |
| After fsync, before the lines were sent | Commands are replayed and take effect | Client heard nothing; on reconnect `WELCOME oseq=` shows the higher number and `RESEND` delivers the events (D73); a resend of the same clOrdId is rejected as DUPLICATE with the original id (D49, within one journal) |
| While compacting | The old journal is still in charge (see 4b) | Nothing visible changed |

**[Verified]** Spec §4 failure model; RESEND path in 05a flow 3.

### 4b. Compaction (`--compact`, D71)

**Trigger:** `java -jar … --journal exchange.journal --compact`, run while the exchange is stopped. **Final effect:** `<journal>.snapshot-<last>` holds the state after the last command; the old journal is copied to `<journal>.archive-<first>-<last>`; the journal is replaced atomically by one that holds only `HEADER,3,<rules>,<last>`. Recovery afterwards costs the snapshot plus the journal since it, not all history.

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Exchange.java:174-193 (trimmed)
public static Compaction compact(Path journalFile) throws IOException {
    try (JournalLock lock = JournalLock.acquire(journalFile)) {
        Replay.Result replay;
        try (CommandJournal journal = CommandJournal.open(journalFile, Rules.VERSION, lock)) {
            replay = Replay.run(journal, journalFile, Long.MAX_VALUE, CommandInterceptor.NONE,
                    Options.defaults().outputRetention());
        }
        if (replay.lastSequenceNumber() == replay.baseSequence()) {
            return null;
        }
        long last = replay.lastSequenceNumber();
        Snapshot snapshot = replay.ledger().snapshot(last, replay.lastOrderId(), replay.lastTimestamp());
        Path snapshotFile = Snapshot.path(journalFile, last);
        snapshot.write(snapshotFile);
        Path archive = journalFile.resolveSibling(journalFile.getFileName() + ".archive-"
                + (replay.baseSequence() + 1) + "-" + last);
        Durable.copyDurably(journalFile, archive);
        CommandJournal.writeFresh(journalFile, Rules.VERSION, last);
        return new Compaction(last, snapshot.orders().size(), snapshotFile, archive);
    }
}
```

1. Take the journal's lock for the whole operation (a running exchange makes it fail: "already open").
2. Replay the journal fully (with its snapshot, CHECKs verified).
3. Write the snapshot: header line (last sequence, last order id, last timestamp, last trade price, digest), one ORDER line per resting order in book priority, one OSEQ line per participant (sorted), END with counts; written to a temporary file, fsynced, atomically renamed, directory fsynced (`Durable.writeAtomically`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/journal/Durable.java:25-36`).
4. Copy the journal to the archive and fsync it.
5. Replace the journal atomically with a header-only journal whose base is `last` (`CommandJournal.writeFresh`, `CommandJournal.java:130-132`).

**Why it is crash-safe:** until step 5 the old journal (with its old base) is untouched and still names its own snapshot, so a crash leaves at most stray new files; after step 5 the new journal names a snapshot that is already durable. Each snapshot's name carries its sequence number, so no file is ever overwritten under a journal that needs it. **[Verified]** code; test `ExchangeTest.Compaction.aCrashAfterWritingTheSnapshotLeavesTheOldJournalInCharge`; **[Inferred]** the general argument.

**What compaction ends:** the client-order-id map keeps only resting orders' ids (an id is unique per participant within one journal, revising D49), and RESEND cannot reach events before it (the snapshot keeps each participant's last oseq, not the events). **[Verified]** `ExchangeTest.Compaction.clientOrderIdsAreScopedToTheJournalExceptForRestingOrders`, `outputNumberingContinuesAcrossACompaction`.

Seen live: `--compact` while the server ran was refused (`Compaction failed: Journal … is already open …`, exit 1); after the stop it printed `Compacted through sequence 4: 0 resting orders in …snapshot-4; old journal archived as …archive-1-4`, and the snapshot read:

```
SNAPSHOT,1,4,3,2026-10-27T14:20:42.045241200Z,100,88a18b6a6365ebe4,d7e6a970
OSEQ,alice,4,d1453b0b
OSEQ,bob,1,3d136969
END,0,2,6a6d3c70
```

**[Verified]** live run.

### 4c. Crossing a rules change

`Rules.VERSION` (now 2) is in every journal header; a journal with records written under other rules is refused, because replaying it under new rules would silently build a different book (D46). The way across (D71): run `--compact` **with the old build** (its journal is now empty, naming a snapshot), then start the new build: `CommandJournal.open` sees an empty journal of other rules, rewrites its header with the new version, and the recovery line says `journal moved from rules N`. The snapshot is state, not commands, so the new rules never re-judge old orders. **[Verified]** `CommandJournal.java:99-105`, `CommandJournal.java:252-258`; tests `CommandJournalTest.Header.anEmptyJournalAdoptsANewRulesVersionAndKeepsItsBase`, `ExchangeTest.Compaction.aRulesChangeIsCrossedWithAnEmptyCompactedJournal`.

---

## Flow 5 — Poison pills, live and at startup

### 5a. Live: quarantine, rebuild from the snapshot, carry on

**Trigger:** within a batch, `ledger.apply` throws a `RuntimeException` on command *k*. **Final effect:** a durable `SKIP` record for *k*; the ledger rebuilt to the state just before *k*; the rest of the batch processed; the caller of *k* gets `INTERNAL_ERROR`; nobody else notices.

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant W as exchange-writer
    participant X as Exchange.process
    participant J as CommandJournal
    participant R as Replay.rebuild
    W->>X: process(batch k-1, k, k+1)
    X->>J: appendAll(k-1, k, k+1) + fsync
    X->>X: apply(k-1): listener, then future
    X->>X: apply(k) throws RuntimeException
    X->>J: append SKIP(next seq, skips k) + fsync
    X->>R: rebuild: snapshot + every command before k, minus skipped
    X->>X: ledger = rebuilt; fail k with INTERNAL_ERROR
    X->>X: apply(k+1) on the rebuilt ledger
```

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Exchange.java:278-287 (trimmed)
private void quarantine(Command poison, RuntimeException cause) throws IOException {
    LOG.log(Level.ERROR, "Engine failed on command " + poison.sequenceNumber() + "; quarantining it", cause);
    journal.append(sequencer.stampSkip(poison.sequenceNumber()));
    skipped.add(poison.sequenceNumber());
    ledger = Replay.rebuild(journal, journalFile, poison.sequenceNumber(), skipped, interceptor, options.outputRetention());
}
```

1. The engine may have thrown half-way through *k*, so the old ledger is discarded, not rolled back. The whole ledger is replaced, so engine, digest and oseq numbering cannot disagree.
2. The SKIP gets the next sequence number *after the whole batch* (the batch was stamped first), with its own fsync.
3. `Replay.rebuild` starts from the journal's snapshot and feeds every command before *k* minus the skip set, in one pass (the journal was verified at open; only this thread has appended since). **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Replay.java:52-57`; test `ExchangeTest.Compaction.aPoisonAfterACompactionRebuildsFromTheSnapshot`.
4. *k*'s outputs were never sent (the listener runs only after `apply` returns), so the rebuilt history is exactly what clients saw. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Exchange.java:235-251`.

An `Error` (out of memory, stack overflow) is not caught here: it propagates and halts the writer (flow 6). **[Verified]** `Exchange.java:239` catches `RuntimeException` only.

### 5b. At startup: stop, then the operator decides

**Trigger:** replay throws `ReplayException(ENGINE_FAILURE, k)` on a command with no SKIP. **Final effect:** startup fails (exit 1) with the hint `Replay failed on command k (a poison pill?). Inspect it, then run --quarantine k and start again.`; after `--quarantine k` the next start skips it.

A failing record is never skipped automatically at startup: the code cannot tell a true poison from a command that succeeded under older code and whose effects clients saw (D51). `Exchange.quarantine` opens the journal (refused while the exchange runs: lock), reads every record, requires *k* to be a NEW, CANCEL or CANCEL_ALL, **refuses a command already quarantined** (L3), and appends `SKIP(last+1, ts, k)`. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/Exchange.java:141-166`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/Main.java:101-107`; tests `ExchangeTest.PoisonPill.*`, `ExchangeTest.Operations.quarantineRefusesACommandAlreadyQuarantined`.

A `DIVERGED` replay is different: no single record is at fault; the build changed. The hint says to compact with the old build first (4c). A record that does not even parse cannot be quarantined: `journal.read` throws on it first, and startup always stops (D38).

---

## Flow 6 — Shutdown, halt, backpressure, timeouts, closing a connection

### 6a. The service state machine

```mermaid
stateDiagram-v2
    [*] --> RUNNING: start()
    RUNNING --> STOPPING: stop() adds the stop marker under the lock
    STOPPING --> STOPPED: writer reaches the marker, closes the journal
    RUNNING --> HALTED: writer throws (journal failure, Error, interrupt)
    STOPPING --> HALTED: writer throws while draining
    STOPPED --> [*]
    HALTED --> [*]
```

"Refused" and "never lost" hold together because the state check and the enqueue share one lock with `stop()`'s state flip and marker (D56). `stop()` refuses to run on the writer thread itself (it would wait for its own end, L6). **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/ExchangeService.java:211-257`.

### 6b. Clean shutdown (Ctrl+C, SIGTERM)

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/Main.java:159-177 (trimmed)
Runtime.getRuntime().addShutdownHook(new Thread(() -> {
    int status = 0;
    try {
        gateway.close();
        if (!service.stop(Duration.ofSeconds(10))) {
            System.err.println("Writer thread did not stop within 10 s");
            status = 1;
        }
    } catch (IOException | InterruptedException e) {
        System.err.println("Shutdown: " + e);
        status = 1;
    }
    if (service.state() == ExchangeService.State.HALTED) {
        status = 2;
    }
    System.out.flush();
    System.err.flush();
    Runtime.getRuntime().halt(status);     // the documented code, whatever started the shutdown
}, "shutdown"));
```

1. Close the gateway: the server socket closes and every session is closed at once (`closeNow`). Each close runs `TcpGateway.removed`, so participants that asked for cancel-on-disconnect get their `CANCEL_ALL` queued now, ahead of the stop marker (05a flow 2b). **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/TcpGateway.java:313-319`; **[Inferred]** COD on shutdown from the order of calls.
2. `stop(10 s)`: STOPPING, marker queued; the writer processes everything accepted before it, then closes the journal (releasing the lock) as its last act and sets STOPPED (D58). **[Verified]** `ExchangeService.java:245-257`, `ExchangeService.java:286-323`.
3. `Runtime.halt(status)` ends the process with the documented code: 0, or 1 if the writer did not stop in time, or 2 if halted. Without it, a JVM ending on a signal reports the signal's status (130 for SIGINT, 143 for SIGTERM). **[Verified]** in a Linux container: exit status 0 after SIGINT and after SIGTERM.

### 6c. Halt

**Trigger:** the writer catches any `Throwable` that escaped `Exchange.process`: a journal I/O error (or the fail-stopped journal's refusal), an `Error`, a failure while appending a SKIP, an interrupt. **Final effect:** HALTED, everyone waiting refused, journal closed, process exits 2. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/ExchangeService.java:306-311`, `ExchangeService.java:342-359`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/Main.java:179-183`.

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/ExchangeService.java:342-358 (trimmed)
private void halt(Throwable cause, List<Pending<?>> batch) {
    LOG.log(Level.ERROR, "Exchange halted", cause);
    synchronized (lock) {
        haltCause = cause;
        state = State.HALTED;           // from here on enqueue() refuses, so the drain below is final
    }
    ExchangeException refusal = new ExchangeException(ExchangeException.Reason.HALTED, "exchange halted", cause);
    for (Pending<?> p : batch) {
        p.fail(refusal);                // no-op for those already completed
    }
    List<Pending<?>> left = new ArrayList<>();
    queue.drainTo(left);
    for (Pending<?> p : left) {
        if (p != stopMarker) {
            p.fail(refusal);
        }
    }
}
```

The batch that failed may be journalled while its callers were told HALTED; on restart those commands replay (durable ⇒ visible does not hold). Clients reconcile with `RESEND` after logging in again (D73) or by resending with the same client order id (D49). **[Verified]** Spec §4.

### 6d. Backpressure and limits

| Refusal | Where | Scope | Journalled? |
|---|---|---|---|
| `BUSY` | `ExchangeService.enqueue`, queue at 100,000 | whole exchange | no |
| `STOPPED` / `HALTED` | `ExchangeService.enqueue` | whole exchange | no |
| `SESSION_BUSY` | `ClientSession.admit`, 1,000 requests in flight | one session | no |
| `RATE_LIMITED` | `ClientSession.admit`, token bucket 1,000/s, burst 2,000 | one session | no |
| slow consumer | `ClientSession.send`, outbound queue of 10,000 lines full | one session, disconnected | no |

**[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/core/ExchangeService.java:222-233`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:116-124`, `ClientSession.java:279-291`. The per-session limits (D75) stop one pipelining client from filling the global queue so that everyone gets BUSY.

### 6e. Timeouts (D75)

The reader's socket read timeout is the login timeout (10 s) until a successful LOGIN, then the idle timeout (300 s; any line, PING included, resets it). On expiry the session sends `ERROR login timeout; disconnecting` or `ERROR idle timeout; disconnecting` and closes after its in-flight requests. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:157`, `ClientSession.java:166-168`, `ClientSession.java:308`; tests `TcpGatewayTest.Limits.aConnectionThatNeverLogsInIsDisconnected`, `anIdleSessionIsDisconnected`.

### 6f. Closing a connection only after its requests complete (D76)

**Trigger:** the reader stops: QUIT, end of stream, an overlong line, a failed login, a timeout. **Final effect:** every request the session handed in completes and its lines go out; then `BYE` (for QUIT); then the connection closes. A broken connection (I/O error) closes at once.

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/gateway/ClientSession.java:421-442 (trimmed)
private <T> BiConsumer<T, Throwable> tracked(BiConsumer<T, Throwable> callback) {
    inFlight.incrementAndGet();
    return (value, failure) -> {
        try {
            callback.accept(value, failure);
        } finally {
            inFlight.decrementAndGet();
            finishIfIdle();
        }
    };
}

private void finishIfIdle() {
    if (readerDone && inFlight.get() == 0 && finished.compareAndSet(false, true)) {
        String last = farewell;
        if (last != null) {
            send(last);
        }
        close();
    }
}
```

How it works: every request a session hands in is counted until its callback has run, and since D76 a callback runs only after the listener has offered the request's lines. When the reader stops it sets `readerDone` and tries `finishIfIdle`; the last callback to bring the count to zero also tries it; the compare-and-set makes exactly one of them close. This also holds during shutdown: requests accepted before `stop()` are still processed, and refused ones complete at once. The D63 version (queue a snapshot read as a barrier) did not, because the exchange refuses reads once stopping. **[Verified]** code; tests `TcpGatewayTest.Session.quitAnswersEveryEarlierRequestBeforeBye`, `endOfStreamStillGetsTheReplies`, `quitDuringShutdownStillGetsTheRepliesOfAcceptedRequests`.

---

## Key takeaways

- Startup = credentials and TLS → bind → accept ("starting") → lock → header check (adopt rules if the journal is empty) → torn-tail cut → snapshot + verify + feed with CHECK comparison → sequencer resume → attach → recovery summary.
- The journal names its snapshot by sequence number; recovery costs the snapshot plus the journal since it.
- `--compact` (exchange stopped) writes the snapshot, archives the journal and atomically replaces it with an empty one; it is crash-safe at every step and is the only way across a rules change.
- A CHECK mismatch (`DIVERGED`) means this build behaves differently from the one that wrote the journal; an `ENGINE_FAILURE` at startup is a poison pill for the operator to quarantine.
- Live poison pill: SKIP (fsynced), rebuild the whole ledger from the snapshot, carry on; the caller gets INTERNAL_ERROR.
- A signal-triggered stop exits 0 (verified on Linux); a halt exits 2; startup and tool failures exit 1.
- Sessions close only after their in-flight requests complete, during shutdown too; refusals at every level are stateless and never journalled.

## Common confusions

- **"Compaction can run next to the exchange."** No: it takes the journal's lock; a running exchange makes it fail.
- **"A snapshot depends on the rules version."** It is state, not commands; that is why it can carry the book across a rules change.
- **"RESEND after a compaction reaches the old events."** The snapshot keeps each participant's last oseq, not the events.
- **"A DIVERGED replay can be quarantined."** No single record is at fault; compact with the old build instead.
- **"Exit code 0 after Ctrl+C is automatic."** It is enforced by the hook's `Runtime.halt(status)`; without it the JVM reports the signal's status.

## Questions to test yourself

1. The journal's header says base 120 and `exchange.journal.snapshot-120` is missing. What happens at startup?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>`Snapshot.read` fails with "Snapshot … is missing: the journal continues from it"; startup stops with exit 1 and the hint "The journal was not changed."</details>

2. A crash hits compaction after the snapshot was written but before the journal was replaced. What does the next start load?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>The old journal, with its old base and snapshot, exactly as before; the new snapshot file is a stray (the next compaction overwrites or reuses it). Tested by `aCrashAfterWritingTheSnapshotLeavesTheOldJournalInCharge`.</details>

3. Commands 10, 11, 12 share a batch; the engine throws on 11. What new record appears, and what state does 12 run against?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>`SKIP,13,<ts>,11,<crc>`. Command 12 runs on a ledger rebuilt from the snapshot plus every command below 11 (minus earlier skips), i.e. the state after 10.</details>

4. You upgrade to a build with `Rules.VERSION` 3. What are the steps, and what does the new build print?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>Stop the old build, run `--compact` with it, start the new build. The empty journal adopts rules 3; the recovery line ends with ", journal moved from rules 2".</details>

5. A client sends NEW and then QUIT while the operator presses Ctrl+C. Does it get its ORDER line?
<details><summary>Answer</summary>If the NEW was accepted before `stop()`, yes: it is processed, the listener sends the ORDER line, its callback brings the in-flight count to zero and only then the session sends BYE and closes (D76). The gateway's `closeNow` during shutdown can still drop lines already queued, since shutdown closes connections.</details>


---

<a id="guide-06-data-and-state"></a>

<!-- source: docs/understanding/06-data-and-state.md -->

# 06 — Data and state

> **Project:** ExchangeServer2 | Single-instrument limit order book: price-time matching engine, write-ahead journal with replay, single writer thread, TCP/TLS gateway with authenticated participants | Stack: Java 17, Maven wrapper, JUnit 5 (+ JMH, PIT)
> **This guide:** The data models (domain, commands, book, engine outputs, core, gateway), every file format (journal, snapshot, users file), the wire protocol, where every piece of state lives and who may touch it, how state changes over time, the validation layers, what persists, and how versions replace migrations | **Prerequisites:** 01, 02 | **Related:** 05, 05a, 05b, 07-modules/*, 13
> **Repo layout summary:** `pom.xml` + Maven wrapper (`mvnw`, `mvnw.cmd`) at the root; production code in `src/main/java/exchangeserver/` (packages `domain`, `book`, `engine`, `command`, `journal`, `core`, `gateway`, `client`, plus `Main`); tests mirror it in `src/test/java/exchangeserver/` (plus `bench/` JMH benchmarks and `testing/MutableClock`); design docs in `docs/` (`ExchangeServer2_Vision.md` = purpose/roadmap, `ExchangeServer2_Spec.md` = decisions D1–D77); these guides in `docs/understanding/`.

## Overview

There is no database. The exchange keeps its state in memory, owned by one writer thread, and persists only **inputs**: a write-ahead journal of stamped commands, plus, after a compaction, a snapshot of the state at one sequence number (D71). Everything else (the book, the counters, the client-order-id map, the output digest, every participant's numbered private stream) is derived by feeding those inputs through the same deterministic engine. A users file holds the participants' salted password hashes (D74). This guide lists every data shape and file format, says where each piece of state lives and which thread may touch it, and how it changes.

Notation: **[Verified]** = read in the code or seen running (cited); **[Inferred]** = reasoning about intent or behaviour.

---

## 1. Data models

### 1.1 Domain (`exchangeserver.domain`)

| Type | Shape | Notes |
|---|---|---|
| `Side` | `BUY`, `SELL`, `opposite()` | |
| `OrderType` | `LIMIT`, `IOC`, `FOK`, `MARKET`, `POST_ONLY`; `canRest()` = LIMIT or POST_ONLY | |
| `Rules` | constants every replayed decision depends on | `VERSION` 2, `MAX_ORDER_QUANTITY` 1e9, `MAX_PRICE`, participant pattern `[A-Za-z0-9_]{1,16}`, client-order-id pattern `[A-Za-z0-9_.-]{1,32}`, `PRICE_BAND_PERCENT` 10, `MAX_OPEN_ORDERS_PER_PARTICIPANT` 10,000, `MAX_RESTING_ORDERS` 1,000,000, `withinBand` |
| `OrderRequest` | participant, clientOrderId, side, type, quantity, price (none for MARKET) | the only field-validation layer (D47); immutable |
| `Order` | orderId, the request's fields, timestamp, mutable `filledQuantity` | built only from an `OrderRequest` |

**[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/domain/Rules.java:21-44`.

### 1.2 Commands (`exchangeserver.command`): what the journal stores

```java
// src/main/java/exchangeserver/command/Command.java:11
public sealed interface Command permits NewOrderCommand, CancelOrderCommand, CancelAllCommand, SkipCommand, CheckCommand {
```

| Record | Fields | Meaning |
|---|---|---|
| `NewOrderCommand` | seq, timestamp, orderId, request | a new order |
| `CancelOrderCommand` | seq, timestamp, participant, orderId (the target) | a cancel |
| `CancelAllCommand` | seq, timestamp, participant | cancel every resting order of one participant (D72) |
| `SkipCommand` | seq, timestamp, skippedSequenceNumber | quarantine an earlier command (D51) |
| `CheckCommand` | seq, timestamp, digest (16 hex) | "the outputs of every command before me digest to this" (D70) |

All are records validated in their constructors; every one gets a sequence number from the `Sequencer`, so the journal numbers 1, 2, 3, … without gaps (only NEW uses up an order id). **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/command/Command.java:11`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/command/CancelAllCommand.java:10`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/command/CheckCommand.java:16`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/command/Sequencer.java:48-76`.

### 1.3 Book (`exchangeserver.book`)

- `OrderBook`: `bids` (`TreeMap` in reverse price order) and `asks`, each price → `PriceLevel`; `orders`: id → `OrderNode` (O(1) cancel); `openOrders`: participant → count; `changed`: a `LinkedHashSet` of touched levels, drained into level updates after each command. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/book/OrderBook.java:41-46`.
- `PriceLevel`: a doubly linked list of `OrderNode`s with sentinels, plus `totalVolume` and `orderCount`; mutators are package-private (D44).
- Read-only copies handed out: `Fill` (what `fillBest` did), `LevelUpdate` (side, price, volume, orderCount; volume 0 = gone), `CancelResult` (`CANCELLED` or `UNKNOWN_ORDER`), `RestingOrderView` (one resting order: id, participant, clOrdId, side, type, price, original, filled, timestamp) for walks, cancel-all and snapshots. **[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/book/RestingOrderView.java:14`, `OrderBook.java:149-171`.

### 1.4 Engine outputs (`exchangeserver.engine`)

| Type | Shape |
|---|---|
| `Response` | orderId, clientOrderId, status (`FILLED`/`RESTED`/`CANCELLED`/`REJECTED`), `reason`, filled, remaining, trades; the constructor rejects contradictions |
| `Reason` | rejects: `DUPLICATE_CLIENT_ORDER_ID`, `PRICE_OUTSIDE_BAND`, `MAX_OPEN_ORDERS`, `BOOK_FULL`, `POST_ONLY_WOULD_CROSS`; cancel: `SELF_TRADE_PREVENTED` (D67) |
| `Trade` | price, quantity, buyOrderId, sellOrderId, aggressorSide, timestamp (the incoming command's) |
| `Execution` | a fill on a resting order, addressed to its owner |
| `EngineOutput` (sealed) | `OrderOutput(seq, ts, response, passiveFills, levelUpdates)`, `CancelOutput(seq, ts, result, levelUpdates)`, `CancelAllOutput(seq, ts, participant, results, levelUpdates)` |
| `BookSnapshot` | the top levels for SUBSCRIBE |
| `EngineState` | lastSequenceNumber, lastOrderId, lastTradePrice, resting orders: what a snapshot stores of the engine (D71) |

**[Verified]** `src/main/java/exchangeserver/engine/Response.java:15`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/engine/EngineOutput.java:14`, `src/main/java/exchangeserver/engine/EngineState.java:14`.
